# Proofs

What the proof steps found: for each proof, the result, the final shape of the API, and any design change. Every step reads this file. Where a result changes a design, the change stands only once the owner approves it, and the entry says which.

## Step 02: proofs 1 to 12

The spikes were built on stable Rust 1.99.1 with no nightly features, in their own workspace under `spikes/`, and run in CI on Postgres 15.19, 16.15, 17.11 and 18.6 in the first runs and on 15.20, 16.16, 17.12 and 18.7 in the last run with the spikes, at `ca3747b`, and on 17.11 and 18.6 locally. Timings below are from release builds, on the CI runners and locally. A pass below means every listed check ran green.

The owner decided DA to DE on the first results, DF1 to DF10 on the independent review, DG1 to DG4 on proof 9's questions, DH1 to DH9 and the parameter-typing design (A to K) on the second review, DI1 to DI4 on the session's zone, DJ2 and DJ3 on the third review, and DK1, DK2 and DK5 on the fourth (`INDEX.md`, Decisions; `reviews/step-02-review-2.md`, `reviews/param-typing-design.md`, `reviews/step-02-review-3.md`, `reviews/step-02-review-4.md`). Each entry below says where a decision changed the design.

Spelling note: the spikes couldn't see the frozen plan, so attribute spellings such as `#[view(Name, hide(..))]`, `#[unique(..)]`, `#[col(group = ..)]` and `#[model(borrowed)]` are the spikes' own. What's proven is the mechanism and the generated shape. The step that builds each feature uses its own step file's spelling.

How to rerun: `spikes/` and `.github/workflows/spikes.yml` were deleted by `9bd63f7` (DE), and the PR's history keeps them; the commits after it change only the plan. The last commit that has them, with proof 9 rerun under DH2, is `ca3747b`: `git checkout ca3747b38fe66e28b579d9bae5e5e9632946049b`, then `SPIKE_DSN=... cargo test --locked --release --manifest-path spikes/Cargo.toml --workspace` and `bash spikes/proof10.sh`, on Postgres 15 or later with `citext` available. `spikes.yml` at that commit runs both on Postgres 15 to 18.

### 1. `#[view]`: pass

`#[model]` reads each `#[view(..)]` on the struct and emits a sibling struct per view, from the model's one field list, with only that view's fields. Each view gets its own `Model` impl (same table, its own `COLUMNS` and `SELECT`) and its own `F`.

- `Product::F` is a `ProductColumns`, `ProductPublic::F` a `ProductPublicColumns`, and a view's columns are typed by the view: `Column<ProductPublic, String, _>`. Passing one where the model's column is expected is a type error naming both.
- A hidden field is absent from the view and its `F`. The error is rustc's own and reads well: `no field 'cost' on type 'ProductPublicColumns'`, with `available fields are: 'id', 'name', 'photo', 'tags'`. A view naming a field the model doesn't have is an error at that name.

### 2. `#[morm::base]`: pass

`#[morm::base]` emits a `#[macro_export]` `macro_rules!` that holds the base's fields as tokens, plus `pub use <that macro> as <Base>;`, so the macro sits at the base's own path. `#[model(base = path)]` calls `path! { @model [args] <struct> }`, and the macro calls `#[::morm::model(__base_fields { .. }, args)]` back with the fields in front. They land as plain fields: a struct literal of the model names them directly.

- A base extending a base asks its parent for the fields once, where it's defined, so its own macro already holds every field. A model using `bases_b::Audit` (which extends `bases_a::Timestamps`) compiles in a crate that doesn't depend on `bases-a`.
- Works across crates, inside the base's own crate, and from a module path.
- `#[macro_export]` puts every such macro at the crate root, so the macro's name carries a hash of the file and line where the base is written (`Span::file` and `Span::line`, stable). Two bases with the same name in one crate both work.
- A unique column declared in a base reaches the model's unique keys (proof 8).

### 3. `Bits<N>`: pass, with two refinements

`groups!` counts its groups (plus the built-in `sudo` at bit 0) and picks `N = ceil(count / 64)`: 4 groups give 8 bytes, 65 give `Bits<2>`, 16 bytes. `#[model(groups(..))]` counts the model's scoped groups the same way, and a model with none has `()` (0 bytes). Generic code works through a `MaskBits` trait implemented for every `Bits<N>` and `()`, so `db.groups(&mask)` is an ordinary generic method on stable, with no `generic_const_exprs`.

- A `Mask<Order>` where `Mask<Product>` is expected, or another model's `ScopedGroup`, is a type error naming both models.
- **Refinement:** `groups!` emits its mask as its own newtype, `pub struct Mask(pub Bits<N>)`, not an alias for `Bits<N>`. With an alias, a group from another `groups!` of the same size would be accepted silently; with the newtype it's a type error (`expected 'app::groups::Mask', found 'app::big::Mask'`).
- **Refinement (DF5): how `type Global` is filled.** `groups!` emits `G` twice: a struct `G`, with one `GlobalGroup<Mask>` field per group, and `pub const G: G`. Types and values live in separate namespaces, so one `use crate::groups::G;` brings in both. It also emits `impl GlobalGroups for G { type Mask = Mask; }`. A model that names a global group gets `type Global = <G as GlobalGroups>::Mask;` from the type, and each group's bit from the constant, `G.admin`. Tested: a model in another crate, `models-a`, that only does `use bases_b::groups::G;`, including one whose fields come back through two base expansions, has `bases_b::groups::Mask` as its `Global`, and `Mask::<Order>::new().with(bases_b::groups::G.auditor)` shows the column that group guards. This is the mechanism under R-0271's plain `use`.
- A model that names no global group gets `type Global = ()`, 0 bytes, and needs no `G` in scope.
- A model that names a global group with no `G` in scope fails with rustc's `cannot find type 'G' in this scope`, pointing at `#[model]`. Step 08 may word it better.

### 4. `async |tx|` closures and `.retry(n)`: pass in the owner's shape (DA, DF2)

**What failed as the plan wrote it.** `.retry(n)` on `AsyncFn` closures: with an `AsyncFn(&Tx)` bound, a closure that only *reads* a captured row by reference isn't `Send` under `tokio::spawn` (`implementation of 'Send' is not general enough`, a compiler limitation with higher-ranked lifetimes), so it would fail in every Axum handler. The review reproduced it without the spike's wrapper closure. And any postfix modifier, `db.transaction(f).retry(n)` or `db.transaction(f).isolation(..)`, needs `IntoFuture` and so a named future: on stable that's a boxed `dyn Future + Send`, and proving the closure's future `Send` for the box takes a bound over every lifetime, which forces captures to be `'static` (`'row.id' does not live long enough ... borrowed for 'static`). Naming the future directly would need `AsyncFnOnce::CallOnceFuture`, which is unstable. The plan's first example, `db.transaction(|tx| async move { .. })`, a plain closure returning an async block, doesn't infer against an `AsyncFnOnce(&Tx)` bound either (`type annotations needed`).

**The shape (owner decisions DA and DF2).** Every transaction option goes before the closure, on one prefix builder, and the closure is always last:

```rust
db.transaction(async |tx| { .. }).await?;                         // AsyncFnOnce(&Tx) -> Result<T>
db.isolation(Serializable).retry(3).transaction(async |tx| { .. }).await?;
db.lock_timeout(d).statement_timeout(d).transaction(async |tx| { .. }).await?;
```

- `db.isolation(..)`, `db.retry(n)` and each per-transaction setting start the builder, and they chain in any order. `transaction` is an `async fn` on it, so its future is `Send` whenever the concrete closure's is, with no box.
- Without `.retry`, the closure is `AsyncFnOnce(&Tx) -> Result<T>`, so it may move or mutate what it captures.
- With `.retry(n)`, it's `AsyncFnOnce(&Tx) -> Result<T> + Clone`, and each attempt runs a copy. A closure that mutates a capture holds a `&mut`, which isn't `Clone`, so it doesn't compile, whatever the order of the options: `the trait bound '&mut String: Clone' is not satisfied ... required for '{async closure}' to implement 'RetryBody<()>'`. A `move` closure gets a fresh copy of its values on each attempt, so nothing leaks from a failed attempt.
- Tested on Postgres 15 to 18, each under `tokio::spawn` with a closure reading a captured row by reference: `.isolation(Serializable)` with `.statement_timeout` and `.lock_timeout` and no retry (the closure also mutates a capture), `.retry(3)` before the other options, and a setting before `.retry(1)`. Inside, `SHOW` reports `serializable`, `1234ms`, `250ms`, `4321ms` and `99ms`, and on the pool's one connection afterwards every setting is back to its default. A retried closure is rerun after a real `40001`, and without `.retry` the `40001` surfaces.

**What `.retry` costs and doesn't catch (DF10).**

- An attempt that could still be retried runs a copy of the closure, and the last attempt allowed runs the original. Measured with a counting `Clone`: `.retry(0)` copies nothing, `.retry(3)` that succeeds at once copies once, and two failures (real `40001`s) copy twice under `.retry(2)` and three times under `.retry(3)`. A closure that borrows its captures copies only references, so it allocates nothing. A `move` closure that owns rows clones them on each copy, so it allocates even on a transaction that never retries.
- Only mutation through `&mut` is refused. State behind a shared reference, in a `Cell`, an atomic or a mutex, carries over from one attempt to the next: the spike's own retry test counts attempts that way.

Also seen: rerunning at once after a serialization failure lost again while the winning transaction was still committing, and a fixed 20 ms pause once let two transactions that had both lost retry in step and collide on every attempt, on Postgres 16 in CI. A pause jittered by the clock's nanoseconds failed the same way once, on 17 in CI. The likely cause: where the clock steps in microseconds, its nanoseconds are a multiple of 1,000, and the pause never varies. The spike now pauses 10 to 50 ms by a fresh random number per pause, from `RandomState`; 80 local runs of its retry tests on 17 and 18 and every CI run since passed. R-0442's jittered backoff covers this, and its jitter must come from a real random source; step 20 tests it (T-0289, DH7). The spike formats `BEGIN` and each `SET LOCAL` into one message per transaction; step 20 chooses how they're sent.

### 5. Hooks: pass, with `#[model(hooks)]` (DB) and two rules (DF3)

- Users write `async fn` in `impl Hooks` against the contract's `fn ..(..) -> impl Future<Output = Result<()>> + Send` methods. A hook that holds a non-`Send` value across an `.await` fails at the hook itself (`future returned by 'pre_insert' is not 'Send'`), not deep inside the web framework.
- **Every contract hook compiles and runs (DF7).** The spike's `Hooks` has the contract's exact signatures: `validate`, `pre_insert(rows: &mut [Self], db)`, `post_insert`, `validate_changes(&Changes<Self>)`, `pre_update(&mut Changes<Self>, &Target<Self>, db)`, `post_update(.., affected: u64, db)`, `pre_delete(&Target<Self>, db)`, `post_delete(.., affected, db)` and `after_commit(Committed<Self>)`. Its `Changes` holds boxed `dyn Param + Send` values and its `Target` holds ids, a list, a filter with parameters, or all rows, so `Send` is checked against realistic contents; the spike's key is `id int8`. A generic update and delete run the hooks the impl overrides, then `after_commit` with `Committed::Updated` or `Committed::Deleted`. Tested: `validate_changes` and `pre_update` refuse before any checkout, `pre_update` adds a change that the statement writes, both update hooks and the statement share one checkout, and the hook log reads `post_update`, `committed update`, `post_delete`, `committed delete`. Axum handlers awaiting a generic insert, and a generic update and delete, compile and serve requests.
- **Marking (owner decision DB).** `#[model]` emits an empty `impl Hooks` for every model, so models without hooks need nothing and run no hook path. A model with hooks says `#[model(hooks)]`, so `#[model]` leaves the impl to `#[morm::hooks]`. `#[morm::hooks]` records which hooks the impl defines in the required `const OVERRIDES`, and the generic paths call only those. An `impl Hooks` written without the attribute doesn't compile (`missing 'OVERRIDES' in implementation`).
- **Rule 1 (DF3): no macro calls in the impl.** `#[morm::hooks]` sees the impl before any macro inside it expands, so a hook written by a macro would go unrecorded and never run. It refuses any macro call among the impl's items: `` `#[morm::hooks]` can't see hooks a macro writes, so they'd never run: write each hook as an `fn` in this impl ``. It also refuses an `OVERRIDES` written inside the impl.
- **Rule 2 (DF3): `Overrides` can't be written by hand.** `Overrides<M>` has a private field, no public constructor and no public constant. `#[morm::hooks]` and `#[model]` build it through a `#[doc(hidden)]` `morm::__private::overrides(&[Hook::PreInsert, ..])`. It's typed by its model, so another model's `OVERRIDES` can't be copied in (`expected 'Overrides<Borrowed>', found 'Overrides<Note>'`). Code asks `M::OVERRIDES.has(Hook::PreInsert)`. Each way around the rules is a pinned compile error, and step 24 builds and tests both rules with a trybuild case each (T-0291, T-0292, DH7).
- The lazy `db` checks out on its first query and keeps that connection: a hook that refuses without a query checked out nothing, and when a hook queries, the statement reuses its connection, one checkout in all.

### 6. Borrowed `ProductRef<'r>`: pass

`ProductRef<'r>` maps `String` to `&'r str`, `Vec<u8>` to `&'r [u8]`, `Option` of either to `Option` of the borrowed form, and keeps other types. A counting allocator measured, over 1,000 rows of text, `bytea` and nullable fields: decoding allocated 0 times, decoding plus `serde_json::to_writer` into the caller's buffer allocated 0 times, and the owned model allocated at least twice per row. Its `Serialize` impl writes the fields directly, with no intermediate value.

### 7. `NestedConnection`: pass, with two refinements

A stream checks out nothing until first polled. On its first poll it registers the polling task, then checks out and runs its query; on each later poll, if the task changed, the registration moves. A checkout reads one relaxed atomic, the count of open streams, and only when it's non-zero looks up the current task. Tested: a query inside a stream loop on a pool of one is refused at once instead of waiting forever; a nested stream is refused; another task may query meanwhile; `join!` of the loop and a query on one task refuses the query; a stream made on one task and polled on another belongs to the second, and moves back when polled on the first; a finished or dropped stream frees the task. The check with no stream open took 0.55 to 1.41 ns.

- **Refinement:** a future driven by `block_on`, including the body of `#[tokio::main]` and `#[tokio::test]`, has no Tokio task id, so the registry keys by task id, or by thread id when there's none. Keyed by task id alone, the loop in `main` deadlocked in the test.
- **Refinement (DF4): the registry is sharded.** While any stream is open anywhere, every checkout on every task looks up its task. Measured with streams open on other tasks, and checks from one thread, then from eight threads at once. With the first spike's one process-wide `Mutex<Vec<..>>`, at commit `1b60b71`, a check took 12 ns and 322 ns with one stream open, 45 ns and 1.07 µs with 100, and 278 ns and 5.4 µs with 1,000 locally (12 threads), and up to 358 ns and 9.5 µs with 1,000 on the CI runners. Sharded 64 ways by a cheap hash of the task, each shard its own mutex and list, it took 8 to 16 ns from one thread and 11 to 30 ns each from eight, from one stream open to 1,000, locally, and 9 to 34 ns and 15 to 116 ns on the CI runners. Step 21 builds the registry, and must not put a single process-wide lock on this path; the sharded form is the measured starting point, and T-0290 measures it with streams open (DH7).

### 8. Upsert conflict targets: pass, with a refinement

Each column's `Column<M, T, C>` has its own zero-sized marker type `C`, generated per field, so each column is a distinct type and can carry its own `UniqueKey<M>` impl. A unique column implements it with `CONFLICT = ("col")`, and `Model::UK.<name>` constants of type `Key<M, marker>` cover expression, partial and composite keys: `(lower("email"))`, `("code") WHERE "deleted" = false`, `("tenant", "code")`. A unique column declared in a base from another crate, reached through two base expansions, is a key too.

- A column with no unique key is a compile error, worded through `#[diagnostic::on_unimplemented]`: ``'Column<Order, String, ..::code>' is not a unique key of 'Order'``, with a note on how to declare one.
- On Postgres 15 to 18, each of the four targets is accepted as the arbiter of `INSERT .. ON CONFLICT .. DO UPDATE`, with a row that conflicts on that key only.
- **Refinement:** the contract's `Cap` parameter becomes the per-column marker type, and capabilities such as refusing `.set()` become traits implemented on the marker, rather than a shared capability type.

### 9. `morm::Param` with `query_typed`: pass with a carrier and a cast for every parameter (DH2)

**The risk the first spike missed.** It took each parameter's declared type from the Rust value, so `String` was always `text`. When the declared type differs from the column's, Postgres casts the column, not the parameter, and the comparison changes meaning with no error. Sent as `text`, `'alice'` found 0 rows in a `citext` column holding `'Alice'`, and `'ab '` found 0 in a `char(5)` holding `'ab'`.

**The design (owner decisions DF1, DG1, DH2 and DH3; `reviews/param-typing-design.md`).** Postgres never infers a parameter's type. Every parameter is declared in Parse as a built-in *carrier* and cast in the SQL as `($n::cast)`:

- **Bound through a field** (builders, `F`, `sql!`'s `${v: M::F.field}`, model inserts and updates), the cast is the column's type, an explicit `sql` included, with no length, precision or scale (`char(5)` casts to `bpchar`, `varchar(5)` to `varchar`, `numeric(10,2)` to `numeric`), and the carrier is that type's built-in carrier. The conversion layer encodes the value as the carrier: a narrower integer as the wider column's, `f32` as `float8`, an instant on `timestamp` as its UTC wall time, a naive time on `timestamptz` as UTC, `IpAddr` on `cidr`, each element alike in a list or an array (DG1). The value must fit the field at compile time (`Fits`, choice K).
- **Free** (`${v: T}`, `.q()`, `set_expr`, `sql_unchecked`), the cast and carrier are the Rust type's own, from `SqlType`: `String` is `($1::text)`.
- **Types with no fixed OID** travel as `text`, or a domain's base type's carrier, and are cast by name: `citext`, an enum, a composite, an extension type, a domain (choice C). No OID is looked up or sent.
- Every carrier is built in, so every parameter goes in binary format. The earlier design's `Type::UNKNOWN`, its text-format rule, DG3's untyped raw strings and lists, and the per-parameter format wrapper are gone. The spike's `Param` has `cast()` and `carrier()` with a blanket impl from `SqlType`, and parameters still go straight from `&[&dyn Param]` into `query_typed_raw` through an iterator of a small `Bound` wrapper, with nothing boxed or collected.
- The parentheses around each cast matter: without them a user's `$1[1]` would read as the type `int8[][1]`. The design session's evidence for that, for index use with casts, and for type swaps under cached statements is in `param-typing-design.md`, section 2; steps 14, 55 and 56 test it on 15 to 18.

**The matrix, rerun under DH2 (choice J).** At `ca3747b`, the last commit of PR #2 with the spikes. Each R-0186 Rust type, against its default column and each column an explicit `sql` can give it, plus an enum, an enum array, a domain over `integer` and a domain over `citext`, inserted and then compared with `=` (or `= ANY(..)` for a list against a scalar column), with the session's `TimeZone` at `Asia/Dhaka` (+06), so a time that moves with the session shows. Each cell two ways: **Field**, bound through the field, and **Free**, cast to its own type. Each runs through `query_typed` and through a prepared statement, which agreed in every cell. Beside each, the findings of a small version of check-sql's typing pass (rules T1 to T3, `param-typing-design.md` section 6), read from the analyzed parse tree Postgres sends back with `debug_print_parse` on. The test fails if a free value's answer differs from the field's and no rule reports it. The table is pinned in the spike, `p09_matrix.expected`, and was identical on Postgres 15.20, 16.16, 17.12 and 18.7 in CI (`spikes.yml` at `ca3747b`, on push and on pull request, with every other spike green) and on 17.11 and 18.6 locally. `morm::Timestamp` (stood in for by `SystemTime`, with the same encoding), `Range<T>` and `Numeric` get their encodings in step 13, which adds them (DC).

| Pairs | Field | Free | Free's findings |
|---|---|---|---|
| Every type on its default column: `i16` `smallint`, `i32` `integer`, `i64` `bigint`, `f32` `real`, `f64` `double precision`, `bool`, `String` and `&str` `text`, `Vec<u8>` `bytea`, `DateTime<Utc>` and `SystemTime` `timestamptz`, `NaiveDateTime` `timestamp`, `NaiveDate` `date`, `NaiveTime` `time`, `Uuid` `uuid`, `Value` `jsonb`, `IpAddr` `inet`, `Decimal` `numeric(10,2)`, `Vec<T>` on `T[]`, `NULL` of each | right | right | none, except `Decimal` into `numeric(10,2)`, T3 (a scale applied) |
| `i16` on `integer`, `bigint`; `i32` on `bigint`; `f32` on `double precision`; `Vec<i32>` `= ANY` on `bigint` | right | right | none: a widening |
| `Vec<i16>` on `integer[]`, `=` the array | right | `42883` | a wrong shape is an error: `int2[]` has no `=` with `integer[]` |
| `String`, `&str` on `citext`, `= 'alice'` | right | wrong: 0 rows | T1 |
| `String`, `&str` on `char(5)`, `= 'ab '` | right | wrong: 0 rows | T1, and T1 on `= 'ab'` too, which is right |
| `String` on `varchar(5)`; too long gets `22001` | right | right | none |
| `Vec<String>` `= ANY` on `citext`, `char(5)` | right | wrong: 0 rows | T1 |
| `Vec<String>` on `citext[]`, `char(5)[]`, `=` the array | right | `42883` | a wrong shape is an error |
| `String` on `json`, `jsonb` | right, `json` keeps the text as given | `42804` on insert, `42883` on `=` | an error |
| `Value` on `json` | right | stored text rewritten (`{"a": 2, "b": 1}`) | T3 |
| `DateTime<Utc>`, `SystemTime` on `timestamp`, and `= ANY` of a `Vec` of instants | right | wrong: stored `06:00`, 0 rows | T3 on insert, T1 on `=` |
| `NaiveDateTime` on `timestamptz` | right | wrong: read as +06, 0 rows | T3, T1 |
| `IpAddr` on `cidr` | right | right | T3 on insert (`inet` to `cidr` goes through a function) |
| `String` on an enum, `= ` a label, `= ANY` of labels; `Vec<String>` on an enum array; a label the enum lacks | right; the missing label gets `22P02` | `42804` or `42883` | an error |
| `i32` on a domain over `integer`, and one breaking its `CHECK` | right; `23514` | right; `23514` | none |
| `String` on a domain over `citext`, `= 'alice'` | right | wrong: 0 rows | T1 |
| `Vec<Option<String>>` on `citext[]` holding `a\b`, an empty string, `{}`, the string `NULL`, a quote and a comma, and a real `NULL`, compared with the same elements in another case | right | `42883` on `=` | an error |

The field never needs a finding: no field-bound cell was wrong, and none drew T1 or T2. Under `TimeZone = 'UTC'`, the zone morm's sessions always use (DH1), every free time row is right, and still reported, T3 on insert and T1 on `=`, because its answer would move with any other zone.

Free values where Postgres has nothing to infer from need no cast of their own, because each carries its own: `($1::text)`, `(($1::text) IS NULL)`, `concat(($1::text), 'x')`, `coalesce`, `(($1::text))::int`, `array_length(($1::text[]), 1)` and `unnest(($1::text[]))` are right, where the earlier design's untyped strings and lists failed with `42P18`, `42804` and `42725`. A wrong shape is an error: `1234::int4 = ($1::text)` gets `42883`, a list where one value is expected, `'x'::text = ($1::text[])`, gets `42883`, and `= ANY(($1::text))` with a scalar gets `42809`. With an untyped literal on the other side, `'x' = ($1::text[])`, Postgres reads the literal as an array and fails with `22P02`.

**What came before, superseded.** DF1 first declared a field-bound parameter as the column's OID, or `Type::UNKNOWN` in text format for a type with no fixed OID, and a raw `String` or `&str` as `UNKNOWN`; DG3 then sent raw text lists the same way. The second review found that a raw list where one value is expected was then silently read as one text value (DH2), and the owner chose the carrier and cast instead. DG1's conversions stay, encoding into the carrier. DG2's `timezone` override is gone (DH1). DG4's wording of R-1104 is replaced by DH2's.

- **DH1: sessions always run in UTC.** morm's connections send `TimeZone = 'UTC'` as a startup parameter, with no setting or variable to change it (R-0060, R-1205, R-1206 in step 10), so raw SQL converts times as morm's own encodings do, and the README says why loudly (R-1210). Behind PgBouncer in transaction mode the parameter was first left out, with the role carrying `timezone = 'UTC'`; since DJ2 it's sent there too (R-0670). Checked with `psql` on 18.6 and 17.11, on servers whose own `TimeZone` was `Asia/Dhaka`: with `-c TimeZone=UTC` as a startup option, `'2026-01-01 00:00:00+00'::timestamptz::timestamp` is `2026-01-01 00:00:00` and `'2026-01-01 00:00:00'::timestamp::timestamptz` is `2026-01-01 00:00:00+00`, where the server's zone gave `06:00:00` and `+06`. A role with `ALTER ROLE .. SET timezone = 'UTC'` starts in UTC, and a startup option wins over it. Wall-clock time as a field type moved to `later-defo-build.plan`.

### 10. Registration: pass, with two refinements

Models in three crates were all found by a stand-in `morm-mgr` built with `--cfg morm_mgr` in its own target folder, including a crate no code references. `cargo build --workspace` without the cfg put no metadata in the app or `mgr`. As a control, the app built with the cfg did carry it, so the search would have found it. Each model registers through `inventory`.

- **Refinement:** a `#[cfg(morm_mgr)]` in `#[model]`'s output warns `unexpected_cfgs` in every crate with a model, which breaks `-D warnings`. So `#[model]` calls `morm::__morm_register!`, a `macro_rules!` that `morm` defines twice, under `cfg(morm_mgr)` and `cfg(not(morm_mgr))`. The cfg is read when `morm` is built, and `morm` declares it in its own `[lints.rust] unexpected_cfgs` `check-cfg`. User crates never see it, and the workspace builds with warnings denied.
- **Refinement:** a crate that `morm-mgr` only lists in `Cargo.toml` is dropped by the linker with its models; tested, its models went missing. The generated `morm-mgr` must name each crate with models, `use <crate> as _;`.

### 11. The loop detector: detection and cost pass, exclusions in step 45 (DD)

Each worker thread keeps eight slots keyed by (task, statement), where a statement is its `&'static str`'s address, so there's no hashing or string compare. The same statement reaching the threshold inside the window on one task is reported once. Nine runs under a threshold of ten, slow repeats outside the window, and one run on each of many tasks are not reported. A task that moves thread starts counting again, which can miss a loop but never invents one. Every query through `Db` passes the detector, and an N+1 loop of twelve queries was reported. The cost was 27 to 47 ns per statement.

**The exclusions (owner decision DD)** are proven in step 45, which builds the detector. The list is R-0785's: `fetch_page`, `.in_batches`, streams, the outbox worker, retries and `morm::allow_repeats(fut)`. Each is either a flag at morm's own call sites or a scope marked while a future is polled, the technique proof 7 proves. Neither changes a step-03 signature, provided step 03's internal query entry point can carry a "don't count this" flag; `contracts.md` says so.

### 12. `deadpool` with our own manager: pass

`deadpool`'s `managed` core with our `Manager` for `tokio_postgres::Client`. `recycle` runs no query; it only checks `is_closed()`. Tested on Postgres 15 to 18:

- Nothing opens until needed, and three concurrent checkouts open three.
- A hundred checkouts opened nothing new and ran nothing on the server: each backend's `state_change` and last query in `pg_stat_activity` were unchanged. A checkout took 0.33 to 0.52 µs, against 40 to 166 µs for an empty round trip.
- `resize` while running: down while idle closes the extra connections at once, down while busy closes them as they come back, and up lets more open.
- Idle shrinking: `retain` on `last_used` closed every connection idle past the limit, on demand and from a timer task, and the pool opened again afterwards.
- Noted for step 11: `retain` allocates a `Vec` per call, so it belongs on the reaper's timer, never on a checkout.

### Owner decisions on step 02

- **DA:** retry is `db.retry(n).transaction(async |tx| ..)` with an `AsyncFnOnce + Clone` closure, each attempt running a fresh copy (proof 4).
- **DB:** a model with hooks is marked `#[model(hooks)]`, with a required `OVERRIDES` const; models without it pay nothing (proof 5).
- **DC:** `Param` binding for morm's own `Timestamp`, `Range<T>` and `Numeric` is proven in step 13, when they get encodings (proof 9).
- **DD:** the loop detector's exclusions, R-0785's list, are proven in step 45, which builds it, provided step 03's internal query entry point can carry a "don't count this" flag (proof 11).
- **DE:** PR #2's last commit deletes `spikes/` and `spikes.yml`; the PR history keeps them.
- **DF1:** a parameter's declared type comes from its column where morm knows it, and raw `String` and `&str` go as `Type::UNKNOWN`; proof 9's matrix (proof 9). The declared type part is superseded by DH2.
- **DF2:** every transaction option goes before the closure on the same prefix builder as retry, the closure always last (proof 4). R-0435, R-0442, R-0453 and R-0767 are rewritten to match.
- **DF3 to DF10:** fixed as the review recommends: the hook rules (proof 5), the stream check measured with streams open (proof 7), the `G` mechanism (proof 3), proof 9's claims, every hook compiled (proof 5), the rerun line above, the open-items summary on `main`, and what `.retry` costs and misses (proof 4, and R-0865's README line).
- **DG1 to DG4:** encode a value as its column's type, times as UTC, and refuse at compile time a `sql` morm can't convert to; connections start with `TimeZone = 'UTC'`, overridable in `morm.toml`; raw text lists go as `UNKNOWN` text and a raw `Value` stays `jsonb`; R-1104 reworded (proof 9). DG2's override and DG3 are superseded by DH1 and DH2.
- **DH1:** sessions always run in UTC, with no `timezone` setting and no `MORM_DB_TIMEZONE`; wall-clock time moves to `later-defo-build.plan` (proof 9).
- **DH2, with the design's choices A to K:** every parameter is declared as a built-in carrier and cast as `($n::cast)`, through the field or free; `sql!` takes values inline (A); `db.sql()` and `db.raw()` take only `sql!` output, and `sql_unchecked` is the escape hatch (B); carriers, not `UNKNOWN` (C); `sql!` refuses a free value beside an `F` column (D); check-sql reads the analyzed parse tree with rules T1 to T4 (E); the scanner writes casts into fragments (F); `get_where` and `delete_where` bind `comp` through its field (G); `#[morm::test]` checks the SQL a test runs (H); R-0718 retries once on any error from a cached statement that casts to a non-built-in type (I); proof 9's matrix rerun in PR #2 (J, proof 9); an `Option` value fits only an `Option` field (K). `sql!`'s core moves to step 16.
- **DH3:** a field's column type is checked through `SqlType`: lossless declarations only, unknown SQL types only on a type with a text form, a compile error naming the field, and `check_schema` checks again at startup (steps 13 and 41).
- **DH4:** step 18 runs the parameter matrix through morm's own query paths, cached statements included (T-0286), and step 45 shows check-sql and `#[morm::test]` report its free mismatches (T-0296); T-0278 runs straight on `tokio_postgres` in step 13.
- **DH5:** `tx.transaction(..)` is a savepoint, `tx` has no `isolation` or `retry`, and a setting on `tx` holds for the rest of the transaction (R-0436, `contracts.md`, R-1216's README notice). Checked on 18.6: a `SET LOCAL` inside a released savepoint holds after it, and rolling back to the savepoint undoes it.
- **DI1:** `check_schema`'s one startup statement also reads `current_setting('TimeZone')` and refuses the pool with `Error::Config` when it isn't `UTC`, which catches a PgBouncer role not set to UTC with no extra round trip (R-1234 in step 41, R-0670, R-1210); since DJ2 it's the backstop for a pooler that drops morm's `TimeZone`. Checked on throwaway 18.6 and 17.11 servers whose own zone was `Asia/Dhaka`, the same on both: `UTC` given as a startup option, or as the role's `timezone`, reads back `UTC`, and so does `utc`; a startup option still wins over the role's `UTC` (`Europe/Berlin`). Other names for the same offset read back as written, not as `UTC`: `Etc/UTC`, `UTC0`, `Etc/Universal`, `Universal`, `Zulu`, `GMT` and `UTC+0`, whether set by startup option or by role, and `+00` reads `<+00>-00`. The owner chose a fixed list of aliases (DI3).
- **DI3: the aliases DI1's check passes.** Only names proven to be a permanent zero offset with no daylight saving and no other history. Both throwaway servers, 18.6 and 17.11, are built `--with-system-tzdata=/usr/share/zoneinfo`, tzdata 2026c. In its source (`tzdata.zi`), `Etc/UTC` is one line, `Z Etc/UTC 0 - UTC`, and `Etc/GMT` one line, `Z Etc/GMT 0 - GMT`: offset 0, no rule, no end date. `Etc/UCT`, `UCT`, `Etc/Universal`, `Universal`, `Etc/Zulu`, `Zulu` and `UTC` are links to `Etc/UTC`, and `Etc/GMT0`, `GMT0`, `Etc/GMT+0`, `GMT+0`, `Etc/GMT-0`, `GMT-0`, `Etc/Greenwich`, `Greenwich` and `GMT` links to `Etc/GMT`. In the compiled files, each family's files are byte for byte the same, and `zdump -v -c -1000,3000` shows no transition in any, with `isdst=0 gmtoff=0`. On both servers, each name set as a startup option reads back in that spelling, shows an offset of 0 and one abbreviation (`UTC` or `GMT`) at every 30 days from 1800 to 2200, and `pg_timezone_names` gives `00:00:00` with `is_dst` false. Postgres reports a zone in tzdata's spelling however it was set (`utc` gives `UTC`, `etc/utc` `Etc/UTC`, `ZULU` `Zulu`, by startup option and by role), so R-1234 compares exactly. Near misses, refused: `Europe/London` showed offsets 0, 3600, 7200 and -75 seconds (LMT) over that span, though `pg_timezone_names` gives it `00:00:00`, not daylight saving, in November, which is why a check by today's offset would be wrong; `Africa/Abidjan` and `Atlantic/Reykjavik` 0 and -968 (LMT); `WET` five offsets; `Etc/GMT+1` -3600. **`UTC0`, one of the five the owner named, fails the tzdata proof:** it isn't a tzdata name but a POSIX TZ string, which Postgres parses itself, so it's absent from `pg_timezone_names`; it showed offset 0 and abbreviation `UTC` over the whole span, as do the POSIX strings `UTC+0` and `+00` (which reads back `<+00>-00`). The other four named, `Etc/UTC`, `Universal`, `Zulu` and `GMT`, pass.
- **DI4: `UTC0`.** The owner chose to accept `UTC0` alone, on the evidence above, as the one POSIX string R-1234 passes; `UTC+0`, `+00` and `<+00>-00` stay refused, and T-0297 pins all three on 15 to 18.
- **DI2:** a `timezone` key in `morm.toml` is refused with `Error::Config`, naming the key and saying sessions always run in UTC (R-1235 in step 10).
- **DJ2: `TimeZone` through PgBouncer.** morm sends `TimeZone = 'UTC'` with `pooler = "transaction"` too, as `options=-c TimeZone=UTC`, the one way `tokio-postgres` 0.7.18 sends a setting at startup (its startup message holds only `client_encoding = UTF8`, `user`, `database`, `options` and `application_name`, all of which PgBouncer tracks or reads); R-1234 stays as the backstop, and the role no longer has to be UTC (R-1205, R-0670, R-1210, R-1234, T-0297, T-0104). Checked on throwaway PgBouncer 1.22.1 and 1.26.1, built from pgbouncer.org's sources, in transaction mode with `default_pool_size = 1`, in front of Postgres 17.11 and 18.6 servers whose own zone was `Asia/Dhaka`, logging in as a role with `timezone = 'Europe/Berlin'`, all four pairs the same: a client sending `-c TimeZone=UTC` reported `UTC`, inside a transaction too, where `'2026-01-01 00:00:00+00'::timestamptz::timestamp` gave `2026-01-01 00:00:00`; a client sending nothing reported `Europe/Berlin`; after that client's `SET timezone = 'Asia/Tokyo'` on the same server connection (the same backend pid), the next `-c TimeZone=UTC` client reported `UTC`, and a client already connected reported `UTC` before and after another client's `SET timezone = 'America/New_York'` in between, while the next client sending nothing reported `Europe/Berlin` again. PgBouncer's source (`set_startup_options` in `client.c`) reads `-c` pairs from `options` and tracks the ones it knows. With `ignore_startup_parameters = TimeZone`, 1.26.1 dropped the parameter, and the session reported `Europe/Berlin`, which is what R-1234 catches; 1.22.1 still kept `UTC`, because before 1.26.1 a tracked parameter won over the ignore list (1.26.1's `NEWS.md`: "Make `ignore_startup_parameters` take precedence over tracked parameters"). The list is compared as written: `timezone` in lowercase didn't drop it on either. `options` in the list changed nothing. A client's own `SET timezone` or `set_config('TimeZone', ..)` becomes that client's tracked zone for its later transactions, as it would on a direct session. The servers and PgBouncers are stopped and their files deleted. CI's pooler job proves it on its PgBouncer (T-0104), and T-0297 the refusal through a PgBouncer that drops the parameter.
- **DJ3: `Sql<'a, P>`.** What `sql!` returns is `Sql<'a, P>`, `P` that query's own tuple of carrier-tagged borrows, so nothing is boxed and a query can be stored, passed around, returned as `-> Sql<'a, impl Params>` and run elsewhere (`contracts.md`, R-1223, T-0299). Proven on stable rustc 1.99.1 with `tokio-postgres` 0.7.18, in a scratch crate standing in for `sql!`'s expansion (a `macro_rules!` writing the nested struct literals a proc macro would), run on throwaway Postgres 17.11 and 18.6: `let q = ..` holding a field-bound `i32` on an `int8` column, a free value `1_i64 + 1`, and a field-bound `String`, run twice through `db.sql(&q)`, once from another `async fn` taking `&Sql<'_, P>`, and once by value, each right; a function `fn by_vendor<'a>(..) -> Sql<'a, impl Params>`'s query ran right; a query built inside `tokio::spawn`, borrowing a captured row, awaited twice, compiled and ran, so the future is `Send`. Under a counting global allocator, building `q` allocated 0 times and binding its three values into a reserved buffer 0 times, and a run through `query_typed_raw` from `Params::bind()`'s array iterator allocated 17 times, exactly as many as the same statement through `query_typed` with a borrowed slice, five runs each after warming up. That `1_i64 + 1` was no evidence for temporaries: it's a constant expression, so Rust promoted `&(1_i64 + 1)` to a `'static`, which needs no extension (the fourth review, DK4). The evidence is the fourth review's run: in the struct-literal form, run-time temporaries `&(x + 1)`, `&(x * 1)` and `&(row.1[0] + 0)`, with `x` from `black_box`, compiled and ran right, and `&(x + 1)` through a function call failed with `E0716`, so a `let`'s struct, tuple and `&` operands extend it and a function call doesn't. T-0299 asks for a run-time value. The crate was deleted with the scratch.
- **DJ4: `Fits<T>` object safe and `Sync`.** In the same scratch crate, `&[&dyn Fits<i64>]` holding an `i64` and an `i32`, held across an `.await` inside `tokio::spawn`, compiled and encoded both (`contracts.md`, T-0283).
- **DK1: PgBouncer 1.20 or later.** PgBouncer accepts the `options` startup parameter from 1.20.0 (its NEWS: "Add support for the `options` startup parameter"); the fourth review's PgBouncer 1.19.1 refused a `-c TimeZone=UTC` client with `FATAL: unsupported startup parameter: options`, and with `options` in `ignore_startup_parameters` dropped it, leaving the role's zone. So morm needs 1.20 or later behind PgBouncer, turns that refusal into `Error::Config` naming both fixes (R-0670, R-0677, R-1237, T-0300), and the README says so loudly.
- **DK2: the `pooler` job.** It runs PgBouncer 1.20, 1.22 and 1.26.1 against Postgres 15 to 18, with a second 1.26.1 instance that drops `TimeZone`, and skips a version it can't install with a `::warning::` naming it and the Postgres version, which the README's contributor section says loudly (R-1238 in step 12; T-0104, T-0297, T-0300). The fix adds a 1.19 for T-0300's refusal case, since no supported version refuses `options`.
- **DK5: `application_name` behind PgBouncer.** morm sends it there too: PgBouncer tracks it, and the fourth review's 1.22.1 and 1.26.1 read back `morm_app` beside `UTC` and `UTF8`, while `-c lock_timeout=5s` in `options` failed with `unsupported startup parameter in options: lock_timeout` (R-0670, R-1205, R-1237, T-0104, T-0300).
- **DK3 and DK6: `Params: Send + Sync`, and 64 values.** The fourth review found a `Conn`-like trait method taking `Sql<'_, P>` by value into a `+ Send` future fails with `` `P` cannot be sent between threads safely `` when `Params` is only `Sync`, and compiles with `Send + Sync`. Checked again in a scratch crate on rustc 1.99.1: with `Params: Send + Sync`, such a method, called from a function generic over the trait with run-time temporaries `&m` and `&(n * 2)` in a stored query, compiled, its future was `Send`, and it gave `61` for `n = 20`; and `Params` implemented for a flat 64-tuple, with `bind()` an array iterator, compiled and bound 64 values. `sql!` refuses a 65th (`contracts.md`, R-1223, R-1224, R-1236, T-0284, T-0299). The crate was deleted.
- **DL1 to DL7 (the fifth review):** R-1238 now ends step 01's apt-to-image fallback, so a version that can't be installed is skipped with a `::warning::`, never run on another version. Its step rewrites `tests/tests/repo_policy.rs`'s R-0792 pooler tests and the stubbed script test for each version's paths, which T-0300 points at (DL1). The skip notice is R-1240, in a "Contributing" section at the end of the README (DL2). The 1.22 and 1.26.1 instances set `max_prepared_statements = 200`, because 1.24.0 changed its default, and 1.19.1 and 1.20.1 run with morm's caches off (DL3). The versions are pinned exactly, 1.20.1, not 1.20.0 with its `options` regression, and 1.19.1, each by SHA-256; pgbouncer.org's `.sha256` files and the downloaded tarballs agreed (`24992cf5…2088` and `58c3eff9…a394`). Each instance checks `SHOW VERSION`, the source builds run in a Debian container pinned by digest, so an Ubuntu mirror outage skips only 1.22, and the second 1.26.1 runs in front of each of Postgres 15 to 18 (DL4). T-0300 covers all four entry points (DL5). R-1236's tuple line is corrected (DL6).
- **DM1 to DM5 (the sixth review):** a skip you can see: `start_pgbouncer.sh` writes each version's result, `ran` or `skipped`, to `$GITHUB_OUTPUT` and ends with no `exit 0` line, each version's test step carries one `if:` on it, and exactly those lines join the `ALLOWED` list of step 01's guard test `nothing_switches_a_job_or_step_off`, while `no_file_under_github_hides_a_failure`, which has no allow list and passes them, doesn't change (DM1, corrected by DN2). Each `if:` compares with `!= 'skipped'`, so a missing or misspelled output runs the step and fails loudly, and the workflow test checks the `if:` lines' step ids and output names against what the script writes (DN1). The stubbed test checks the checksum warning names the file and both hashes, and stubs `sudo`, `systemctl` and `pgbouncer` for the 1.22 path (DN3). A tarball whose SHA-256 doesn't match is skipped with a `::warning::`, like a failed install, and the stubbed test drives it (DM4, owner decision; the Contributing notice says so in both copies). `SHOW VERSION` is matched as it prints, `PgBouncer 1.20.1`; the stubbed test stubs `psql`; the container runs PgBouncer as a non-root user, because PgBouncer refuses root (1.20.1's and 1.19.1's `src/main.c`, per the review); `make` joins the build inputs (DM2). A second 1.19.1 instance runs with `ignore_startup_parameters = options` for T-0300's workaround (DM3). The PR body (DM5). All in R-1238, R-1240 and T-0300.
- **DK4, DK7 to DK9:** T-0299 asks for a value computed at run time; `-> Sql<'a, impl morm::Params>` gets its "use this when" line (R-1239); T-0297's `check_schema = "off"` case runs through the PgBouncer that drops the parameter; the PR body.
- **DJ1 and DJ5 to DJ11:** fixed as the third review recommends: every integer type morm maps, `u128` on `uuid`, `char`, `NonZero*` and `Vec<T>` in `SqlType`'s table (R-1207, R-1213, T-0278, T-0280); only text types have a text form (R-1208, R-1233); the README lines R-1236 and R-1210's "What you give up"; `db.check_schema()` checks the zone, and a `timezone` key is refused in every table (R-1234, R-1119, R-1235, T-0298); T-0286 sets a non-UTC zone on a held connection; this file's matrix row, rerun line and versions; T-0290's bound.
- **DH6 to DH9:** fixed as the review recommends: `Param`'s comment, now the carrier design's; T-0289 to T-0292 for the rules handed to steps 20, 21 and 24; loud README notices R-1210, R-1211, R-1215 and R-1216, and `.statement_timeout(...)`'s line R-1217; the PR body.

## Step 55: proofs 13 to 18

The spikes were built on stable Rust 1.99.1 with `tokio-postgres` 0.7.18, in their own workspace under `spikes/`, and run in the temporary `spikes.yml` on the `postgres:15` to `postgres:18` images, Postgres 15.20, 16.16, 17.12 and 18.7 (PGDG Debian builds), with PgBouncer 1.22.0 (Ubuntu 24.04's apt package) and 1.26.1 (CloudNativePG's image, pinned by the digest `ci.yml` uses), a streaming standby from the same image, and `postgresql-NN-plpgsql-check` 2.10.16-1.pgdg13+2 from PGDG (extension version `2.10`). Proofs 13 to 15 and 17 also ran locally on 18.6 and 17.11, with the same results, apart from the German messages, since the local 17.11 is built without translations.

Every printed row was the same on all four versions, apart from three things: OIDs (printed as `<n>`), the standby's replay timings, and the internal routine Postgres names for two errors, `ProcessInterrupts` on 15 and 16 against `ProcessRecoveryConflictInterrupt` on 17 and 18 for a recovery conflict, and `set_config_option_ext` against `set_config_with_handle` for a refused `SET`. A pass below means every check the spike asserts passed on every version. Proofs 14 to 17 record what Postgres does, and their tables are what the spikes printed.

Logs: the run at `757361d`, on push (https://github.com/biggaloy/rust-morm/actions/runs/43421574293) and on the pull request (https://github.com/biggaloy/rust-morm/actions/runs/43421580485), each job named `spikes (pg NN)`, one step per proof. GitHub keeps them for 90 days, so everything they show is written here.

How to rerun: the commit that deletes `spikes/` and `spikes.yml` names its parent, the last commit that has them, in this file's last line. Check that out, then `SPIKE_DSN=... cargo run --locked --release --manifest-path spikes/Cargo.toml --bin p13` (and `p14`, `p15`), against a superuser on Postgres 15 or later; `spikes.yml` at that commit shows the extra setup proofs 14 (a German locale), 16 (`spikes/start_pgbouncers.sh`), 17 (`spikes/start_standby.sh`) and 18 (the PGDG package) need.

| # | Proof | Result |
|---|---|---|
| 13 | Enum and composite binding with no OID | Pass, with an owner-approved change: the driver's own lookup of an enum or composite result column fails behind PgBouncer with no prepared statements, so morm reads such columns as `::text` (EA) |
| 14 | Stale statements after a type swap | Recorded, with an owner-approved change. With the old type kept, a statement that touches no table of the type runs on the old type with no error for a label both types share, which after a reorder is a wrong answer (EE1); the reset and the relabel fix it (SA). Every message but `XX000`'s is translated, so stale errors are recognised by SQLSTATE and routine (SC) |
| 15 | Compiled plpgsql after a swap | Recorded, with an owner-approved change. `DISCARD PLANS` cures `%ROWTYPE` and the trigger, not `DECLARE v st` or `SELECT ... INTO`, against R-0994's text (EE7); with the old type kept, `DECLARE` and `INTO` fail with `22P02` or run on the old type; the relabel makes them fail loudly, and a no-op `ALTER` recompiles unmanaged ones (SA, SB) |
| 16 | Stale state behind PgBouncer | Recorded, with an owner-approved change. **It contradicted R-0718 and R-0981 behind PgBouncer**: re-preparing, and replacing the client connection, don't clear a stale server session. The reset, the shared statement generation in the cached text and an unnamed retry fix it (SA, SD, SE) |
| 17 | Stale state on a hot standby | Recorded, with an owner-approved change for a session the replay ends inside a transaction (SG). The standby behaves as the primary does, the reset and the relabel replay, and every recovery proved in 14 and 15 works on it |
| 18 | `plpgsql_check` | Pass, with an owner-approved change. An in-comment `@plpgsql_check_options: relid = ...` hides an `error`, so step 59 refuses functions containing it, as the step says; `disable:performance_warnings` and `disable:check` can hide a narrowing (SH) |

The owner answered Q1 to Q4, and the independent review's EE1 to EE10 (`reviews/step-55-review.md`) and EE11 (`reviews/step-55-open-items.md`), as EA and SA to SH: EA on the review, and SA to SH as `reviews/stale-statement-design.md` recommends, every choice, with EE7 to EE9 fixed as the review recommends; these are owner decisions, which the coordinator records in `INDEX.md`. The second review (`reviews/step-55-review-2.md`, EF1 to EF9) asked for the design's evidence in CI, so the spikes came back for one more run, with a new spike, `p19`, and new rows in proof 18 (EF9; see "Re-run in CI for the second review" below). Before that, the design session ran its evidence locally on Postgres 15.20, 16.16, 17.12 and 18.7, built from ftp.postgresql.org's tarballs checked by SHA-256 with `--enable-nls`, behind PgBouncer 1.20.1 and 1.22.1 built from pgbouncer.org's tarballs checked by SHA-256, each with `default_pool_size = 3`, `min_pool_size = 3` and `server_round_robin = 1`, on a streaming standby of each made with `pg_basebackup -R`, `hot_standby_feedback = off` and `max_standby_streaming_delay = 3s`, and with `plpgsql_check` 2.10.16 built from its `v2.10.16` tag (SHA-256 `b4734ffac4110348e8b963abb5b4f97cf0ade819ab92a74f0ddd47c4141288e3`), through `psql` and a throwaway harness on `tokio-postgres` 0.7.18 that casts every parameter with its carrier. The review ran its own checks on 18.6 and 17.11, and behind PgBouncer 1.20.1 and 1.22.0. Each result below says whose run it was, and all four versions gave the same rows unless it says otherwise. Every owner-approved change is written into the step files, `contracts.md`, `coverage.md` and `tools/manifest.json` in PR #3, and the steps that build each change keep permanent tests of it.

### 13. Enum and composite binding with no OID: pass, with an owner-approved change (EA)

The shape, which steps 13 and 56 build on:

- **Enum, `ToSql`:** `accepts` only `Type::TEXT`, and writes the label's bytes. The SQL casts it: `($2::"p13"."order_state")`. A list is a `Vec<T>` bound with the `text[]` carrier and cast `($3::"p13"."order_state"[])`.
- **Enum, `FromSql`:** `accepts` `Type::TEXT` or any `Kind::Enum(_)`, ignoring the variant list inside it, and matches the label's bytes to a variant. A label it doesn't know is an error (R-0979's `UnknownEnumValue`). `Vec<T>` decodes an enum array through it.
- **Composite, `ToSql`:** the text form `("street",zip)`, with `"` and `\` escaped by `\`, in a `text` carrier, cast `($4::"p13"."address")`; a list is `text[]` cast to `"p13"."address"[]`.
- **Composite, `FromSql`:** `accepts` any `Kind::Composite(_)`, and reads the binary record (a field count, then each field's OID, a length and that many bytes, where a length of `-1` is a NULL field with no bytes), skipping the OIDs on the wire and never reading the driver's field list. The spike's decoder didn't check a length against the buffer, so a NULL field would have panicked it; the test data had none (EE9).
- **What steps 13 and 56 build, after EA:** enum and composite result columns are read as `::text`, so `PgEnum`'s and `PgComposite`'s `FromSql` accept only `text`, and a composite is parsed from its text form, which, on 18.6, Postgres prints as `("a,b",)` for `a,b` and a NULL, `("",1)` for an empty string, `("a\\b""c",2)` for `a\b"c` and `(" x",3)` for a leading space. Nothing between delimiters is NULL, and every position and length is checked, with an error, never a panic, on bad input (EE9; R-1241, T-0301). `ca::text[]` on a composite array returns each element's text form, so lists need no array-literal parsing.

What ran, on a connection opened at the start and on one opened in each phase: an insert of every column (enum, enum array, composite with a quote, a comma and a backslash in its text, composite array) through `query_typed` and through a prepared statement, then each row read back through five selects (an enum parameter, an enum array parameter with `= ANY`, a composite parameter, a composite array with `= ANY`, and every column read as `::text`), each through `query_typed` and a prepared statement, decoded and compared with what was written: 111 checks per version, all passing. Phases: at the start; after `ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE 'shipped'`, binding and decoding the new label; after a swap of both types in one transaction (enum: rename, create with the labels reordered plus `refunded`, `ALTER COLUMN ... TYPE ... USING s::text::"order_state"`, drop the old one; composite: rename, create again, `USING addr::text::"address"`, drop the old one), binding and decoding `refunded`.

- `pg_prepared_statements` shows the parameters declared as `{text,integer}` and `{text[],integer}`: no enum or composite OID reached the server.
- After `ADD VALUE`, the driver's own copy of the enum on the first connection still lists `new,paid`, while `shipped` decodes: the decoder never reads it.
- Statements the first connection prepared before the swap and that return the swapped columns get `0A000` after it; one returning them as `::text` keeps working. Proof 14 covers these.

**Finding (Q1), and the owner's answer (EA).** `tokio-postgres` itself looks up any result column type that isn't built in. When a row holds an enum or composite column, it prepares and runs a query on `pg_type` by OID, then one on `pg_enum` for an enum's labels or on `pg_attribute` for a composite's fields, once per connection per OID, as named prepared statements of its own (seen in `pg_prepared_statements`: one statement each on the first connection), on the prepared path and through `query_typed` alike (`prepare.rs` and `query.rs` call `get_type` for any OID `Type::from_oid` doesn't know). A connection that reads the same columns as `::text` prepared none. The spike recorded this as a cost and recommended testing it in step 12, because through PgBouncer 1.22 and 1.26.1 with `max_prepared_statements = 200` the lookup worked; it ran with `default_pool_size = 1` and never behind a PgBouncer that keeps no prepared statements.

**It's a failure, not a cost (EE2).** The lookup prepares and executes its statements in separate round trips, which in transaction mode with no prepared-statement support can reach different server connections. The review ran eight clients, five rounds, three `query_typed` decodes of an enum column each, 120 decodes per setup, on 18.6 and 17.11:

| PgBouncer | Pool size | `max_prepared_statements` | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1.20.1 | 4 | n/a | 83 of 120 failed with `26000 prepared statement "sN" does not exist` (`FetchPreparedStatement`) |
| 1.20.1 | 1 | n/a | 120 right |
| 1.22.0 | 4 | 0 | 86 of 120 failed the same way |
| 1.22.0 | 4 | 200 | 120 right |

**EA, owner decision:** morm reads enum and composite result columns, and arrays of them and domains over them, as `::text` or `::text[]` in every column list it writes, so the driver never looks their types up, and raw SQL must cast too: `PgEnum` and `PgComposite` decode only `text`, an uncast column is `Error::SchemaMismatch` naming the cast, and `check-sql` and `#[morm::test]` report one (R-1241 in step 56, R-1242 in step 16, R-1243 in step 45, T-0301 to T-0304, R-1254's README notice). The pooler job runs pools of at least three server connections (R-1238, T-0104), and T-0304 reads such columns behind PgBouncer with no prepared statements. Two choices of this fix, which the owner approves through PR #3: a type morm doesn't know, such as `citext` or `ltree`, under a field with a text form is read as `::text` too, because the driver looks it up the same way; and a scalar domain over a built-in type needs no cast, because Postgres sends the base type in the row description, as this fix checked on 18.6 (a domain over `text` came back as OID 25, a domain over an enum as the enum's OID). **That isn't so for an array of a domain (EF2):** the second review found on 18.6 and 17.7 that `posint[]`, `dtext[]` and `denum[]` come back as their own array types, with OIDs of 16384 or more, so the driver looks them up; `SELECT d::int4[]` read `{1,2}`. So `SqlType`'s `READ_AS` gives a list of a `PgDomain` over a built-in type its base array's cast, such as `"col"::int4[]`, which keeps the binary decoder, and T5 reports an uncast array of a domain (R-1241, R-1243, T-0301, T-0303, T-0304, `contracts.md`). The pooler job also gains a 1.26.1 instance with `max_prepared_statements = 0`, the configuration EE2 named, so T-0304 runs the failing setup on a supported PgBouncer and not only on 1.20.1 (EF4, R-1238).

**Statements cached before `ADD VALUE` (EE8).** The spike ran the statements it kept only after the swap. The review ran three statements prepared before `ADD VALUE 'z'`, with the new label, on 18.6 and 17.11: a free `($1::st)::text`, an `INSERT` binding `z`, and a `SELECT s FROM t` returning the column. All three returned `z` with no error. T-0148 now runs them.

### 14. Stale statements after a type swap: recorded, with an owner-approved change (SA, SC)

Each table `t` holds `s st` and `ss st[]`, and the swap renames `st` to `st_old`, creates `st` with the labels in another order plus `d`, changes both columns with `USING s::text::st`, inserts row 4 holding `d`, and then drops `st_old` or keeps it, in one transaction from another connection. Before it, each statement was prepared with its carrier (`int4`, `text` or `text[]`) and run once, on one connection run outside a transaction and another with each run in its own `BEGIN` ... `ROLLBACK`. After it, each ran three times (twice with the new label `d`, once with the old `b`), was prepared again on the same connection, ran through `query_typed`, and ran on a new connection. The same on 15, 16, 17 and 18, in and out of a transaction:

| Cached statement | Old type dropped | Old type kept |
|---|---|---|
| Returns the column: `SELECT s FROM t WHERE id = ($1::int4)` | `0A000` `cached plan must not change result type` (`RevalidateCachedQuery`), every run | the same |
| On the swapped table: `s = ($1::st)`, `s = ANY(($1::st[]))`, `ss @> ARRAY[($1::st)]`, an insert and an update, each returning `s::text` or ids | right, every run, the new label included | right |
| No table of the type: `SELECT ($1::st)` | `XX000` `cache lookup failed for type <n>` (`getTypeInputInfo`), every run, the old label too | `22P02` `invalid input value for enum <schema>.st_old: "d"` (`enum_in`); the old label `b` runs on the old type with no error, which the spike read as right only because it round-trips `b` as text (EE1) |
| `SELECT ($1::st)::text` | `XX000` the same (`getTypeOutputInfo`) | `22P02` the same |
| `SELECT ($1::st[])::text[]` | `XX000` the same (`getTypeInputInfo`) | `22P02` the same |

- Every failure repeated on every run until the statement was prepared again; a re-prepare on the same connection, `query_typed` and a new connection were all right. The old statement kept failing after the re-prepare, since it's still cached. This section said R-0718 and T-0151 held as written; they don't, with the old type kept (EE1, below).
- The step's line "Postgres 18 gave `0A000`, `XX000` ..., `42883` and `42804`" wasn't reproduced for cached statements: with carriers, no statement gave `42883`, and `42804` came only from plpgsql. The design session's `42883` was from plain inference (`param-typing-design.md`, section 2) and its `42804` from plpgsql, which this matches.
- **A `%ROWTYPE` function compiled before a column changes from `integer` to `bigint`** fails with `42804` `type of parameter 4 (bigint) does not match that when preparing the plan (integer)`, routine `plpgsql_param_eval_recfield`, on every call, on 15 to 18. A new connection clears it, as R-0981 says. `DISCARD PLANS` on the same session clears it too, and `DISCARD ALL` does.
- **`lc_messages` (Q2).** With `SET lc_messages = 'de_DE.utf8'` (a superuser, on a server with the locale and the PGDG translations), on 15 to 18: `42804` reads `Typ von Parameter 4 (bigint) stimmt nicht mit dem überein, als der Plan vorbereitet worden ist (integer)`; `0A000` reads `gecachter Plan darf den Ergebnistyp nicht ändern`; `22P02` reads `ungültiger Eingabewert für Enum p14_stmt_keep.st_old: »d«`, still naming the type. `XX000` `cache lookup failed for type <n>` is never translated: Postgres raises it with `elog`. The routine field (`plpgsql_param_eval_recfield`, `RevalidateCachedQuery`, `enum_in`, `getType...Info`) is never translated. A role that isn't superuser can't choose for itself: `SET lc_messages` and the startup option `-c lc_messages=...` both fail with `42501`. This section said no client could change it, which overstated it (EE3): the review found on 18.6 and 17.11 that a superuser can run `ALTER ROLE app SET lc_messages = 'C'`, or, on 15 and later, `GRANT SET ON PARAMETER lc_messages TO app`, after which the role can `SET` it or send it at startup.

**With the old type kept, a stale statement runs on the old type with no error (EE1).** Postgres records no plan dependency on a plain enum type, so a cached plan keeps the old type's OID, and while the old type is kept, as R-1050 keeps it after every online swap until `purge`, a label both types share runs on it. After a reorder that's a wrong answer, and a cached `INSERT ... WHERE` writes a row the new order excludes. The review showed it on 18.6 and 17.11 (`st` `('a','b','c')` swapped to `('c','b','a','d')`, `st_old` kept): `EXECUTE b_lt('a','c')`, `($1::st) < ($2::st)`, returned `t` where a fresh statement returns `f`; `b_audit('c')` inserted its row; a plpgsql `DECLARE v st` function returned `true`. The design session ran SA's swap (rename, create reordered with `d`, retire the column, change a column to `bigint` in the same step) on 15.20, 16.16, 17.12 and 18.7, straight, with the same results on all four:

| After the swap | No relabel, no reset | Relabel only | Reset only | Relabel and reset |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `EXECUTE b_lt('a','c')`, `($1::st) < ($2::st)` | `t`, **silent** | `22P02` naming the retired type | `f`, right | `f`, right |
| `EXECUTE b_const`, `'a'::st < 'c'::st` | `t`, **silent** | `t`, **silent** | `f`, right | `f`, right |
| `EXECUTE b_audit('c')`, an `INSERT ... WHERE 'a'::st < ($1::st)` | inserts the row, **silent** | `22P02` | inserts nothing, right | inserts nothing, right |
| A function `RETURN ('a'::st < 'c'::st)` | `true`, **silent** | `true`, **silent** | `false`, right | `false`, right |
| A function with `DECLARE v st; v := p;` comparing `v < 'c'::st` | `true`, **silent** | `22P02` naming the retired type | `42883 operator does not exist: st_old < st` | `22P02` naming the retired type |
| A function with two such variables, comparing `v < w` | | | `true`, **silent** | |
| A `%ROWTYPE` function | `42804` (`plpgsql_param_eval_recfield`) | `42804` | right | right |
| `EXECUTE` of a statement returning the changed column | `0A000` | `0A000` | `0A000` | `0A000` |

- **The relabel** renames each label of the kept type to `_morm_retired_<label>` (since the owner's answer to the twenty-second fix round's second question, `_morm_retired_<random>_<label>`, R-0995). Rows keep every value, since Postgres stores a label by its OID, and read as the placeholders. It alone isn't enough: Postgres folds a constant such as `'a'::st` into a cached plan as the label's OID, so a folded comparison keeps the old order.
- **The reset** creates and drops an operator in `_morm`. `plancache.c` registers `PlanCacheSysCallback` on the `pg_namespace` and `pg_operator` syscaches on 15 to 18, and the callback runs `ResetPlanCache()`, which marks every saved plan invalid, protocol-level statements and plpgsql's expressions included, in every session of the database at commit. Creating and dropping a schema and `GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA` reset too, and `ALTER SCHEMA ... OWNER TO` its current owner didn't. `DISCARD PLANS` in the session gave the same table as the reset. It alone isn't enough either: a plpgsql variable's type is fixed when the function compiles, so `DECLARE v st` stays on the old type.
- **Both together** leave nothing silent. A role owning the type and a schema for the operator, not superuser, ran the type rename, the relabel and the operator pair in one transaction on all four versions. With `lock_timeout = '200ms'` they committed while another session sat idle in a `REPEATABLE READ` transaction that had read the table. `pgbench -M prepared -S`, 8 clients, 10 s, on 18.7: 176,149 and 167,832 tps with no reset, 164,899 with one every 0.2 s. Once the retired type was dropped, as `purge` does, the `DECLARE` function in an old session gave `XX000 cache lookup failed for type`, which R-0981 already handles. SQL-language functions over the changed table were right after the reset.
- **A transaction open at the cutover.** Postgres applies a committed catalog change to an open transaction only when it next takes a lock: in a `READ COMMITTED` transaction begun before the swap, a cached `($1::st) < ($2::st)` returned the old answer after the swap committed, until the same transaction read another table (all four versions). It ends with the transaction, and a transaction that touches a swapped table can't straddle the cutover, which takes that table's lock.

**SA, owner decision:** every migration `tx` step ends with the reset and raises the statement generation (R-1248 in step 33); a retired enum type's labels are renamed `_morm_retired_<label>`, down and rollback map them back through the ledger, and labels starting `_morm_` are refused (R-0995, R-1049, R-1050, R-1023, R-0977, R-0997, R-1001); README notices R-1249 and R-1251. R-0718 and T-0151 change to match.

**SC, owner decision (Q2, EE3):** stale errors are recognised by SQLSTATE with the routine, an identifier or an untranslated `elog` message, never translated text, with English messages as a second signal (R-1245, R-0981, R-0983, R-0984; T-0151 and T-0152 pin each routine on 15 to 18, in German too). The design session found, on 15.20 to 18.7 with German messages: `pl_exec.c` raises the parameter-type `42804` from exactly three routines, `plpgsql_param_eval_recfield`, `plpgsql_param_eval_generic` and `plpgsql_param_eval_generic_ro` (a `record` variable after a composite is made again gave the last); `RevalidateCachedQuery` raises the `0A000`, and 18 also raises its message from `sql_postrewrite_callback`, which isn't a stale statement; a retired type's name is in the `22P02` message in every language. The option of requiring English messages wasn't chosen, so its README notice isn't written.

### 15. Compiled plpgsql after a swap: recorded, with an owner-approved change (SA, SB)

Four functions, each compiled in a session (called once) before the swap, then called after it with the new label `d` twice and the old label `b` once: `DECLARE v st` assigned from a `text` argument, `SELECT s INTO v` with `v st`, a `t%ROWTYPE` variable, and a `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger function that never names the type (`IF NEW.s IS DISTINCT FROM OLD.s`). The same on 15 to 18:

| Function | Old type dropped | Old type kept |
|---|---|---|
| `DECLARE v st` | `XX000` `cache lookup failed for type <n>` (`getTypeInputInfo`), every call | `22P02` `invalid input value for enum <schema>.st_old: "d"`; the old label `b` works |
| `SELECT ... INTO v` | `XX000` (`getBaseTypeAndTypmod`), every call | `22P02` the same; `b` works |
| `%ROWTYPE` | `XX000` (`getTypeOutputInfo`), every call | `42804` `type of parameter 4 (<schema>.st) does not match that when preparing the plan (<schema>.st_old)` (`plpgsql_param_eval_recfield`), every call |
| The trigger | `XX000` (`format_type_extended`), every write | `42804` `type of parameter 14 (...st) does not match ... (...st_old)` (`plpgsql_param_eval_recfield`, from the spike's log, EE3), every write |

What clears it, each in its own session compiled before the swap:

- `DISCARD PLANS`, and `DISCARD ALL`: cure `%ROWTYPE` and the trigger; `DECLARE` and `INTO` keep failing (`XX000`, now from `typeOrDomainTypeRelid` for `DECLARE`, or `22P02`). The step expected `DISCARD PLANS` not to help at all, and R-0994 said it doesn't clear a stale compiled function; it clears some shapes and not a variable of the type (EE7). R-0994 now says so.
- A no-op `CREATE OR REPLACE` of each function from another session: cures all four in a session that was failing.
- A fresh session: all four right.

**Q4, and the owner's answer (SB).** With the old type kept, `DECLARE v st` and `SELECT ... INTO v` fail with `22P02` naming `st_old` for a label the old type lacks, and keep the old type for labels both types share. The kept type is the default state after every online swap until `purge`, not an edge case, and always dropping the old type would conflict with R-1050 (EE5). The design session found, on 15.20 to 18.7:

- A `DECLARE v st` function compiled before the swap kept failing on every call, through named, unnamed and simple-protocol statements, until something recompiled it (18.7: 12 calls through the harness and 6 through `psql`). After the relabel each failure is `22P02 invalid input value for enum <schema>._morm_retired_m2_st: "a"`, routine `enum_in`. The reset doesn't fix it, and neither do `DISCARD PLANS` or `DISCARD ALL`.
- **A no-op `ALTER FUNCTION f(text) COST 100`**, the cost unchanged, run by the function's owner, not superuser, from another session, made the next call in the failing session compile the function again and return the right answer, on all four versions: Postgres recompiles a plpgsql function when its `pg_proc` row's `xmin` or `ctid` changes, and any `ALTER FUNCTION` writes a new row. Behind PgBouncer 1.20.1 and 1.22.1 it fixed every server session, and on each standby, once replayed. A procedure has no `COST`: this fix checked on 18.6 that `ALTER PROCEDURE pr(text) COST 100` fails with `invalid attribute in procedure definition`, and that `ALTER PROCEDURE pr(text) SECURITY INVOKER`, on a procedure already `INVOKER`, writes a new row (its `xmin` and `ctid` changed), as `ALTER FUNCTION ... COST` does; R-1039 uses that for procedures, a choice the owner approves through PR #3.

**SB, owner decision:** managed functions keep R-1039's refresh; each unmanaged plpgsql function or procedure the walk finds that the migrating role owns gets the no-op `ALTER`; a `22P02` naming a `_morm_retired_` type is a stale session; R-1040's decision says which it refreshed, that morm apps straight to Postgres recover by themselves, and that behind PgBouncer you run `RECONNECT` (R-1039, R-1040, R-0981, T-0168, R-1250).

### 16. Stale state behind PgBouncer in transaction mode: recorded, contradicted R-0718 and R-0981, owner-approved change (SD, SE, SF)

Proofs 14 and 15 repeated through PgBouncer 1.22.0 and 1.26.1, `pool_mode = transaction`, `max_prepared_statements = 200` (as morm runs 1.22 and later), and `default_pool_size = 1`, so every client reaches the same server session, as it can in production. The migration ran straight on Postgres. `server_reset_query` is `DISCARD ALL`, which PgBouncer doesn't run in transaction mode (`server_reset_query_always = 0`). The same on both PgBouncers and all four Postgres versions:

- **What the client sees** is what proofs 14 and 15 saw straight: `0A000`, `XX000` or `22P02` for the same cached statements, and the same plpgsql errors, on every run.
- **Re-preparing doesn't clear a stale statement**, on the same client or on a new one: PgBouncer keeps one server-side statement per query text and hands the new prepare the old one, so the stale error comes back, sometimes at the prepare itself (`prepare: 0A000`, `prepare: XX000 ... (getBaseTypeAndTypmod)`). R-0718's evict, re-prepare and retry, and R-0981's retry on another connection, would both fail again.
- **What clears a stale statement:** the same query with any change to its text (a trailing `/* g2 */` was enough): every statement was right at once; an unnamed statement (`query_typed`): always right; PgBouncer's `RECONNECT`, after which a new client's prepare was right.
- **Replacing the client connection doesn't clear plpgsql either:** a new client reached the same server session and got the same errors. `DISCARD PLANS` and `DISCARD ALL` sent by the client cured `%ROWTYPE` and the trigger, not `DECLARE` or `INTO`, as straight. What cleared all four: a no-op `CREATE OR REPLACE` from the migration's connection, or `RECONNECT`. The `%ROWTYPE` column-change case (`42804`) was cleared by `DISCARD PLANS` and by `RECONNECT`.
- An enum column decodes through both PgBouncers, so the driver's own type lookup works there (proof 13). With `default_pool_size = 1` every round trip reached the one server, which hid EE2's failure (proof 13), and a marker counted inside each process starts at the same value in every process, so a process started after a swap would prepare the same text and get the stale server statement back; these errors also arrive at prepare time, which R-0718 didn't cover (EE4, EE11).

The design session repeated it behind PgBouncer 1.22.1 with `max_prepared_statements = 200` and three server connections: six clients each cached S1 (`SELECT ($1::st)::text`), S2 (`(($1::st) < ($2::st))::text`), S3 (`SELECT n FROM t WHERE id = ($1::int4)`, whose result type changes), S4 (a `t%ROWTYPE` function), S5 (a `DECLARE v st` function, never refreshed) and S6 (an `UPDATE` firing a trigger comparing `NEW.s`) under the marker they read (`g1`), ran them until every server session held them, then SA's swap ran straight on Postgres. Each cell is six calls, the same on all four versions:

| After the swap, with the reset | S1 | S2 | S3 | S4 | S5 | S6 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Old clients, their cached statements | right | right | `0A000` | right | `22P02` retired | right |
| New clients preparing the old text (`g1`) | right | right | **`0A000` at prepare** | right | `22P02` retired | right |
| New clients preparing under the marker they read (`g2`) | right | right | right | right | `22P02` retired | right |
| Old clients, the same text unnamed | right | right | right | right | `22P02` retired | right |
| After the no-op `ALTER FUNCTION` on S5's function | | | | | right | |

| After the swap, without the reset | S1 | S2 | S3 | S4 | S5 | S6 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Old clients, cached | `22P02` | `22P02` | `0A000` | `42804` | `22P02` | `42804` |
| New clients, old text | `22P02` | `22P02` | `0A000` at prepare | `42804` | `22P02` | `42804` |
| New clients, a marker nothing raised (`g1`) | `22P02` | `22P02` | `0A000` at prepare | `42804` | `22P02` | `42804` |
| Old clients, unnamed | right | right | right | `42804` | `22P02` | `42804` |

Behind 1.22.1 with `max_prepared_statements = 0` and behind 1.20.1, where morm runs every statement unnamed, every call was right with the reset apart from S5, until the `ALTER`; without it, S4 and S6 gave `42804` and S5 `22P02` to old and new clients alike. Straight to Postgres, with the reset only S3 (`0A000`, R-0718's re-prepare) and S5 (`22P02`, a new connection or the `ALTER`) failed. So with the reset the only stale server statement left behind PgBouncer is one whose result type changed, permanent for that text on that server connection and arriving at prepare as often as at execute, and a marker every process reads cures it once a migration raises it.

**SD, SE and SF, owner decisions (Q3, EE4, EE11b):** behind a pooler with the caches on, cached text starts with `/* morm g<n> */`, `n` the statement generation in the `_morm` schema's comment, which every `tx` step raises and every pool reads at start; a stale signal at prepare or execute retries once unnamed, then adopts the new `n` or, when it didn't move, a random salt; a stale-session signal behind a pooler raises no connection generation; the statement generation rises only for a confirmed stale statement, and a `22P02` is confirmed only by a retry that succeeds, because its text can come from a client, which the owner approved. The second review showed that the retry rarely confirmed anything, because a stale session's retry landed on another pooled connection that had compiled the function too, and that a client could still make morm throw away one connection per request (EF1). **EF1, owner decision:** when no bound parameter's text holds `_morm_retired_`, which no `PgEnum` label can, morm retries once on a newly opened connection, and a success discards the first and raises the connection generation; when one does, it's a client's label, and the error is returned at once, with no retry, discard or generation (R-0981, R-0982, R-1245, R-0984, R-1040, R-1249, R-1250, T-0152, T-0153, T-0311, T-0312). A pool with `check_schema = "off"` still reads the generation at start, with its own catalog query (EF5). Requirements: R-1246, R-0718, R-0981, R-0982, R-0975, R-1116, R-1248, T-0306, T-0311, T-0313, T-0171 and R-1253.

### 17. Stale state on a hot standby: recorded, with an owner-approved change (SG)

The swaps ran on the primary, and every app session was on a streaming standby (`hot_standby = on`, `hot_standby_feedback = off`, `max_standby_streaming_delay = 5s`), waiting for replay before each step. The same on 15 to 18:

- Cached statements and compiled functions on the standby give exactly what proofs 14 and 15 recorded on the primary, dropped and kept (inserts, updates and the trigger don't run on a standby).
- **Recovery on a `read_only` pool:** a re-prepare on the standby, a new standby session, and a no-op `CREATE OR REPLACE` run on the primary and replayed each cured what they cure on the primary; `DISCARD PLANS` and `DISCARD ALL` likewise cure only `%ROWTYPE`. So the recovery proved in 14 and 15 works on a standby, with the refresh run on the primary.
- **Replay cancels queries.** Replaying a swap's `ALTER TABLE` needs an exclusive lock on the standby. A query reading the table was cancelled after the 5 s delay with `40001` `canceling statement due to conflict with recovery`, and replay then went on.
